--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of results:**
- Champion (4941.93): 3-state FSM with two Attraction states + one Stop state. The stop state (s2) has a transition back via floor=WHITE condition (p=0.63), which is problematic since the floor is gray. The green block detection (C=7, l=2 for green) appears in s1 with p=0.4652 targeting state 0. But the stop state should be triggered by seeing green (C=7, l=2 targeting the Stop state).
- Score 5031.78: 4-state with Stop state (s1) - transitions back via floor=WHITE. 
- Score 5061.46: Pure Stop state - scores 5061 because robots are always stopped (bad in phase 1).

**Key insight:** The champion uses C=7 (perceive color) with l=2 (green) to transition, but it goes from state 1 to state 0 (another attraction state), not to the stop state. The stop state (s2) has only a floor=WHITE condition to leave, which is essentially a lock-in since floor is gray (p=0.63 chance each step to leave - but since floor is GRAY not WHITE, this barely fires). Wait - if floor is gray, C=2 (WHITE) fires with probability p when floor IS white... but floor is gray, so it won't fire. This means state s2 is essentially a terminal stop state! That's why the champion works - once in s2, it stays stopped.

The champion needs the green detection to go TO state s2 (stop). Looking again: s1 has transition n1x1 going to state 0 (destination 0 among others [0,2]) when it sees color 2 (green). That goes back to s0 (attraction), not stop. The stop transition from s1 (via floor conditions) must be doing something. Actually the stop state s2 is reached via floor=WHITE from s0 or s1 - since floor is gray, these fire with probability p each step. So robots gradually drift into s2.

**Better design:** Moving states should detect green (C=7, l=2) and transition to Stop. The stop state should have very low escape probability.

**Strategy:**
1. Elitism: copy champion exactly
2. Exploitation: variants of champion with green detection going TO stop state
3. Exploration: cleaner 2-state and 3-state designs with explicit green→stop transitions

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 2 results:**

The champion is a clean 2-state FSM: Exploration (rwm=30) → Stop, triggered by seeing green (C=7, l=2, p=0.95) or a tiny random probability (0.002). The stop state has a tiny escape probability (0.001) keeping it essentially terminal.

Score 4082.04 is good but the question is: can we do better?

**Key observations:**
1. The champion uses `--n0x0 0` (destination 0, which with 2 states means the OTHER state = state 1). Wait - with 2 states, leaving state 0, the only other state is state 1, so D=0 means state 1. So seeing green → stop (state 1). ✓
2. The stop state has destination 0 → state 0 (exploration) with p=0.001, rarely escaping.
3. Score 4136: GoToColor(green) + Exploration + Stop - slightly worse but similar structure.
4. Score 4264: Attraction instead of Exploration - slightly worse.

**What can improve:**
- The random escape from stop (p=0.001) costs ~1.2 points over 700 steps per robot. Making it even smaller could help.
- The false-positive rate from C=7 (p=0.95 seeing green when not present) - if robots accidentally see green-like objects and stop early in phase 1, that costs points. But p=0.95 means once green IS visible, 95% chance per step to transition.
- Could try p=0.99 for the green detection (faster stop after signal).
- The rwm=30 turning rate - try variations.
- The tiny random stop probability (0.002) - if this fires during phase 1, it costs points. Remove it or make it smaller.
- GoToColor(2=green) as moving state: robots actively seek the green wall, which helps them detect it faster and stop sooner. Score 4136 uses this as first state.

**Strategy:**
1. Elitism: copy champion
2. Exploit: vary p for green detection (0.99, 0.9), vary rwm, reduce escape probability
3. Try GoToColor(green) as primary moving state (robots rush toward green wall once it appears)
4. Try 3-state with GoToColor(green) intermediate state
5. Try removing the stochastic stop (only green detection)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 3:**
The champion uses **GoToColor(green)** as the moving state - robots actively move toward the green wall. When they see green (C=7, l=2), they transition to Stop with p=0.95. This is brilliant because:
1. During phase 1: no green wall exists, so robots keep moving toward the green-colored wall (if any) or just move around
2. When the green wall appears (phase 2): robots see it, move toward it, and quickly stop

The score dropped dramatically from ~4082 to ~2574 by using GoToColor(2) instead of Exploration.

**What to try:**
- The stochastic escape (p=0.001) in stop state is nearly zero - keep it
- The p=0.001 random stop during moving is very small - good
- Vary the green detection probability (0.95 seems good, try 0.99)
- Try adding a second transition from moving state (e.g., neighbor count)
- Try GoToColor with different color parameters (but green=2 is what we want)
- The stop state currently has p=0.001 escape - try making it even smaller or zero (but need at least 1 transition)
- Try 3-state with GoToColor(green) → exploration → stop
- The 3-state version scored 2958 which is worse, so 2-state seems better
- Try varying vel (fixed at 1.0), try different clr in moving state

**Exploitation:**
- Champion with p=0.99 for green detection
- Champion with p=0.999 for green detection  
- Champion with slightly different random stop prob
- Champion but stop state exits only via green detection (not random)

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: GoToColor(green) + repulsion + stop
- 2-state: GoToColor(green) with multiple transitions
- Using GoAwayColor during phase 1 (away from non-green?)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 4:**
The new champion (2399.18) has an interesting twist: the **Stop state** exits via green color detection (C=7, l=2, p=0.05). This means if a stopped robot still sees green, it has a 5% chance per step to re-enter the GoToColor state. This seems counterproductive... but wait - maybe it helps robots that stopped BEFORE the green wall appeared (due to the stochastic p=0.001) to eventually restart and stop again properly. Or perhaps it allows fine-tuning of position near the green wall.

Actually, thinking more carefully: during phase 2, the green wall IS visible. Robots stop, but with p=0.05 per step they might restart. That would COST points in phase 2. Yet this scored better (2399 vs 2574). Perhaps the benefit is: robots that accidentally stop during phase 1 (via p=0.001) can escape via the green condition (which won't fire since there's no green in phase 1), or more likely the stop→restart helps robots that stopped early in phase 2 to reposition closer to green and stop more reliably.

Wait - actually I think the key is that p=0.05 for seeing green in the stop state means robots that CAN see green but stopped early will restart and move closer. Once they can no longer see green (they're very close to the wall?), they stay stopped. This is a form of "approach and stop at green wall."

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Vary p for the stop-state green exit: 0.02, 0.05, 0.1, 0.2
- Vary p for the move-state green detection: 0.95, 0.99
- Vary the random stop probability: 0.001, 0.0005
- Try adding a second transition in stop state

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: GoToColor(green) → something → stop, where stop has green exit
- Try stop state with both green exit AND tiny random exit

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Pattern analysis:**
The champion structure is clearly converging: GoToColor(green) → Stop, with the stop state having a small green-triggered escape. The key parameters being tuned:
- `p0x0` (green detection probability in moving state): 0.95 seems good, 0.9 and 0.99 both slightly worse
- `p0x1` (random stop probability): 0.001 seems good
- `p1x0` (green-triggered escape from stop): **0.01 is best**, 0.02 next, 0.05 and 0.1 worse, 0.2 much worse

The trend: lower escape probability from stop is better, but 0.01 beats 0.001 (pure stop). This suggests the green-triggered restart helps robots that accidentally stopped (during phase 1) to restart without costing too many phase-2 points.

**Why does green-triggered escape from stop help?**
- During phase 1: no green wall, so C=7(l=2) rarely fires → robots stay stopped (bad), but they only got there via p=0.001 random, which is very rare
- During phase 2: green wall IS visible, so stopped robots see green and with p=0.01 per step restart... but this costs points! 
- Wait, maybe what actually helps: if a robot stops (randomly) BEFORE phase 2, it can't see green (no green exists), so it stays stopped (costs phase 1 points). When phase 2 starts, the green appears and now the robot can exit the stop state and move toward green... but then it sees green again and stops immediately (0.95 chance). Net effect: the robot moves a tiny bit near the green wall, which counts as "moving" and thus costs phase 2 points.

Actually I think the p=0.01 escape helps because it provides a small correction mechanism. Let me try even smaller values like 0.005, 0.007, and also try different combinations.

**Exploitation:**
- Try p1x0 = 0.005, 0.007, 0.008, 0.015 (fine-tuning around 0.01)
- Try p0x0 = 0.95 with various p1x0
- Try adding a second transition in stop state (random + green)
- Try p0x1 = 0.0005 (even rarer random stops during phase 1)

**Exploration:**
- Try GoAwayColor(non-green) as moving state
- Try 3-state with a more careful structure

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Pattern analysis:**
The champion (2288.07) is essentially converged on the 2-state GoToColor(green)→Stop structure. The scores in round 6 are very close (2383-2406 for near-identical machines), suggesting we're near a local optimum.

Key parameter findings:
- `p0x0` (green detection → stop): 0.95 seems optimal (0.93, 0.97, 0.99 all slightly worse)
- `p0x1` (random stop): 0.001 seems good, 0.0005 slightly worse in round 6 (2383 vs 2288)
- `p1x0` (green exit from stop): 0.01 is best, 0.005 close, 0.007/0.008 untested precisely
- Adding a second transition to stop state hurts (2693)
- Removing the random stop transition hurts (2881)
- 3-state versions are consistently worse

**What to try now:**
1. Fine-tune around the champion: try p1x0 = 0.009, 0.011, 0.012 (very fine)
2. Try p0x0 = 0.94, 0.96 (between tested values)
3. Try p0x1 = 0.0008, 0.0015 (between tested values)
4. Explore genuinely different topologies since we're near convergence:
   - What if the moving state has a neighbor-count transition in addition to green detection?
   - What if we use GoToColor with a different backup moving strategy?
5. Try the stop state exiting via both green AND a tiny random (but we saw that's worse)

The biggest unexplored area: does changing the random stop probability (p0x1) interact with the green escape (p1x0) in a useful way?

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Pattern analysis:**
We're converging tightly. The champion uses p0x0=0.93, p0x1=0.001, p1x0=0.008. The top 5 performers are all very similar 2-state GoToColor(green)→Stop machines with small variations.

Key observations:
- p0x0 (green detection probability): 0.93 seems slightly better than 0.95; try 0.90, 0.91, 0.92
- p0x1 (random stop probability): 0.001 seems good; 0.0008 close; 0.0015 worse; 0.0005 worse
- p1x0 (green exit from stop): 0.008 best, 0.007 close, 0.009/0.010/0.012 slightly worse, 0.005 worse
- The sweet spot seems to be p1x0 ∈ [0.007, 0.010], p0x0 ∈ [0.93, 0.95], p0x1 ≈ 0.001

**Fine-tuning strategy:**
- Try p0x0 = 0.90, 0.91, 0.92 combined with p1x0 = 0.008
- Try p0x0 = 0.93 with p1x0 = 0.006, 0.007, 0.009 (around the best)
- Try p0x1 = 0.0009, 0.0012 (around 0.001)
- Try p0x0 = 0.93, p0x1 = 0.0008 (combining best p0x0 with best p0x1 from round 7)

**Exploration:** Since 2-state is consistently winning and 3-state is worse, let me try one genuinely different approach: what if GoToColor(green) detects green AND the stop state has a very small random exit instead of green exit? This was tested before (scored ~2561) so it's worse. The green-exit from stop IS helping.

Let me focus heavily on exploitation with fine parameter tuning.

## Reasoning

**Pattern analysis from Round 8:**
The champion is now at 2217.59 with p0x0=0.93, p0x1=0.001, p1x0=0.010. Interestingly, the previous champion had p1x0=0.008 but now 0.010 wins. The top performers all use p0x0=0.93 and p0x1=0.001, with p1x0 varying: 0.010 (best), 0.006, 0.007, 0.009 all close.

The "worst" performers in round 8 are still very good scores (2247-2284) - the whole population has converged tightly.

**Key findings:**
- p0x0 = 0.93 is consistently best (0.94 slightly worse, 0.90-0.92 untested with p1x0=0.010)
- p0x1 = 0.001 is best (0.0008 and 0.0012 both worse)
- p1x0: 0.010 won this round, but 0.006-0.009 are very close. Try 0.011, 0.012, 0.013 (going slightly higher)
- All scores are within ~70 points of each other - very tight convergence

**Strategy:**
- Focus on fine-tuning p1x0 around 0.008-0.012 with p0x0=0.93
- Try p0x0 = 0.91, 0.92 with best p1x0
- Try p0x1 slightly varied: 0.00095, 0.00105
- Try a few exploration candidates with genuinely different approaches (escape local optimum)

**Note on scoring:** The scores are noisy (stochastic simulation), so the "best" from round 8 might just be a lucky run. The true optimum likely lies in the region p0x0∈[0.91,0.95], p0x1≈0.001, p1x0∈[0.006,0.013].

## Reasoning

**Final round analysis:**
We're in round 10 (last round). The champion has p0x0=0.93, p0x1=0.001, p1x0=0.012. The scores are extremely tight (2213-2269 range across all machines this round).

**Parameter landscape:**
- p0x0 = 0.93 is clearly best (0.91 worse, 0.92 not tested recently)
- p0x1 = 0.001 is best (0.00095/0.00105 close, 0.0009 worse)
- p1x0: 0.012 won, 0.006 close (2223), 0.008 (2229), 0.013 (2238), 0.011 (2250), 0.015 worse (2269)
  - The best range seems to be 0.006-0.013, with 0.012 winning this round
  - But scores are noisy - 0.006 scored 2223 which is close to 0.012's 2213

**Strategy for final round:**
- Focus tightly on the champion neighborhood: p0x0=0.93, p0x1=0.001, p1x0 ∈ {0.010, 0.011, 0.012, 0.013, 0.014}
- Try p0x0=0.92 with best p1x0 (0.012)
- Try p0x1=0.001 with p1x0=0.012 (multiple evaluations to reduce noise)
- Try a couple of genuinely novel machines as exploration bets
- The champion must be included verbatim

Given high noise, submitting the champion multiple times (with tiny variations) increases probability of finding a better score through luck of the draw.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. This mission has TWO SUCCESSIVE PHASES separated by a signal, and the scoring rule is REVERSED between them. The score is MINIMISED.

PHASE 1 - BEFORE THE SIGNAL. At the start of each run the loop function draws a secret switch time uniformly between 400 and 600 simulation steps. Until that step, the score gains +1 for every robot that is STATIONARY. During phase 1 the robots must KEEP MOVING.

PHASE 2 - AFTER THE SIGNAL. Exactly at the switch time, one randomly chosen wall block of the arena turns GREEN. From that step on the rule flips: the score gains +1 for every robot that is MOVING. The robots must STOP and stay still for the rest of the run.

A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. Stopping immediately is a BAD strategy: it costs about one point per robot for each of the ~500 steps of phase 1. Moving for the whole run is equally bad, for the ~700 steps of phase 2. The machine needs at least one moving state and one stopped state, and the transition into the stopped state must be driven by PERCEIVING THE GREEN BLOCK. A transition based only on a fixed probability cannot be accurate, because the switch time is redrawn at every run. The floor is uniformly GRAY, so floor conditions carry no information in this mission.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 8 walls, inner radius 0.84 m. 10 e-puck robots start scattered inside a disc of radius 0.53 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.75 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots, an inner radius of 0.91 m and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
